The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to enclose five copies
of the text of an announcement made to the press by Mr. J. C. TwImim,
an official of the Price Control and Rationing Division of the Eoonomis
and Scientific Section of this Headquarters, concerning improved dis»
tribution of supplemental food items to coal miners in Hokkaido. Such
items have been allocated for use in coal-mining areas by the Japanese
Government as incentives to inspire increased coal production.
Kr. Twinem stated that "local food committees...and other organ-
izations must make mining communities realize the joint responsibility
of labor, management and government to insure that preferential rations
result directly in more coal being produced". He announeed that
corrective measures have been taken in Hokkaido to insure that supple
mental food rations are given only to miners who do a full dayts work
OE
or achieve individual production quotas, and that absentee miners are
A-S
no longer receiving supplemental rations. In addition, he stated that
ED
underground workers are to receive more incentives than surface workers.
(It is understood that there is an excess number of surface workers in
proportion to underground workers.)

SCAP OFFICIAL LECLARES CORRLCTIVE RATIONING
MEASURES HAVE BEEN TAKEN IN HOKKAIDO
Corrective measures have been taken in Hokkaido to insure
that supplemental food rations go only to miners who do a full
day's work or achieve individual production quotas, a SCaP
rationing official said today.
J. C. Twinem of the Price Control and Rationing Division,
Economic and Scientific Section, who recently returned as a
member of the special fact-finding production team in Hokkaido,
said these measures were taken by accompanying Japanese food and
clothing representatives.
The official said there was no shortage of food for Hokkaido
coal miners on a district level, but that distribution of
supplemental items, especially miso, shoyu and fish, could be
improved within mines and mining localities.
"Local focd committees are doing a gocd job in correcting
inequalities in distributirn," Mr. Twinem said. "They and other
organizations must make mining communities realize the joint
responsibility of labor, management and government to insure
that preferential rations result directly in more coal being
produced."
The cfficial listed four recent improvements of staple
supplementary food rations in Hokkaido:
l-Rationing differentials were revised in some instances
to insure that underground workers receive more food than
surface workers.
2-Office workers and those in occupations not directly
related to digging coal, instead of receiving supplemental
rations of miners, now receive supplemental rations specified
for their particular class of work.
3-Indiscriminate distribution of hard tack and biscuits
intended only for underground workers no lnger exists.
4-Through cooperation of Prefectural Governor Tanaka,
absentee miners no longer receive.supplemental raticns.
Price Control and Rationing Livision officials said
Jepanese ESB and Coal Board teams have now returned to Toky
with additional information from individual mines to be used
by the Japanese Government in improving food, clothing and ther
incentive goods programs benefiting all Japanese miners.

TYPHOON "JEAN" LIOVING WEST OF YAP
Typhoon "Jean" was centered 30 miles southwest of Yap at
9 a.m. today, moving west at 10 miles per hour, the 43d weather
Wing announced. "Jean" is expected to be centered 18o miles
north-northwest of Palau by 9 a.m. tomorrcw. Maximum surface
winds near. the center of the typhoon are 90 miles per hour,with
little change in intensity forecast in the next 24 hour period.
Heavy rains over a lo0 mile radius accompany the typhoon.
High tides and swells are fcrecast along the east. coast of the
Philippines for the next 48 hours.

November 26, 1948
My dear Dr. Evans:
Bill Sebald, who is our United States Political Adviser
for Japan, has indicated to me in a recent letter that a
complete file of the Mainichi Shimbun (Japansse edition) from
June 17, 1917 to date 1s available for purchase in Tokyo.
The collection is said to be in excellent condition except
for very slightly singed edges caused by fire during the B-29
raids. The asking price is around 400 or 500 thousand yen
which at the exchange rate of about 300 yen for one US dollar
would mak the colleotion cost about a thousand dollars to an
American purchaser.
I am impressed with the importance and value of this
colloction and it occurred to me that The Library might be
interested in aoquiring it. As I know of several universities
which are interested in the collection, would. you be good
enough to let me know at the earliest possible date whether
the Library would like to purchase it.
Sincerely yours,
Frank A. Schulor, Jr.
Chief, Japan-Korea Branch
Public Affairs Overseas Program Staff
Dr. Luther H. Evans, Librarian,
The Library of Congress,
Washington, D. C.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Subject: Japanese Reaction to Fall of Mukden.
POLICY PLANNING
893.00
STAFF
The Honorable
DEC 10 1948
The Secretery of State,
894.
Washington.
895.00
Department of State
C2
Sir
893.00
I have the honor to invite the Department's
attention to some examples of Japanese reaction to
the Chinese Coamunist capture of Mukden and the general
deterioration of the position of ti.e Chinese Nationalist
Government.
For some time this mission has noted increasing
-448
concern among Japanese over the intensification of
communist activity in the Far East and its possible
repercussions on Japan. Considereble Japanese attention,
particularly in the press, has been directed to the
gradual consolidation of the communist position in
AY 61949
Manchurie and North China, the advent of the so-called
"People's Republic" in North Korea, increasing communist
activity in South Korea, and the attempted communist
coup at Madioen in Indonesia. The collapse of Chinese
Nationalist resistance at Mukden, coming so close after
the serious comaunist uprising in South Korea, has
created a marked feeling of uneasiness among Japanese.
These are developments exceedingly close to the Japanese
homeland and they stimulate a growing fear of communist
encirclement. As several Japanese newspapers have com-
mented, the Korean uprising and the fali of Mukden have
created a situation which can no longer be regarded as
"a fire on the opposite side of the river"(kawamuko no
kaji, a common expression to signify that a matter is
of no concern).

te
Gil

class sheet written
Jωs
6400

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 709,
November 4, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
In its issue of October 30, 1948 the conservative
Tokyo Shimbun, in a somber article under a title
admonishing preparati.1 for a conflagration on the
continent, warned the Japanese that the fire now raging
on the continent threatens to raze the whole Far East,
and that there is no security that these raging flames
will be prevented from spreadins over Japan. Moreover,
the Tokyo Shimbun emphasized, the Japanese communists
are preparin  the way for the spread of the continental
conflagration to the Japanese homeland.
The Dai Ichi Shimbun of October 30 declared that
the Japanese people cannot remain indifferent to the
red crisis on the continent, for "asia is now the battle-
field on which decisive bettles are likely to be fought
by the two opposing powers--Communists and Anti-Communists."
Japan, therefore, stands at the crossroads, and although
at present Japan is "forging ahead under the strong leader-
ship of the United States toward sound democracy", there
is a strong comunist unlercurrent in Japanese life which
is directly connected with the "red cyclone" raging on
the continent. Luch as the Dai Ichi Shimbun favors
continued United States aid to assist China in defeating
the communists, it is pessimistic as to how effective
such aid can be at the present time, and expresses the
fear that further military aid to China may only convert
the Asiatic cold wr to a najor conflict into which Japan
will be drawn.
In its issue of November 1 the influential Tokyo
Asahi noted with deep pessimism the fact that the Nationalist
forces have suffered defeat after defeat notwithstanding
the large amount of financial and material aid received
from the United States. The destiny of China, Asahi
concludes, has a profound bearing on the destiny of the
other countries of Asia, but with the fall of Mukden
"the activities of the Chinese communists will henceforth
have graver significance than ever before".
Another large circulation daily, the Tokyo Mainichi,
devoted considerable space to the Manchurian situation
in its issue of November 2. This newspaper sees in the fall
of Mukden a vast intensification of communist activity
throughout the Far East and fears that the bolshevization
of the Asiatic mainland will have a malignant effect on
Japan's economic rehabilitation. It concludes that in
control of Manchuria, the Chinese communists will be able
to exercise influence over the strategic area of northeast
Asia which formerly played such an important role in the
economic life of Japan.
The English-language Nippon Times for November 3
editorially described Manchuria as the door to Korea
"which was once referred to as a dagger pointed at the
heart
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's. Despatch No. 709,
November 4, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
heart of Japan," and expressed the belief that the
communization of Lanchuria "presents a menace of major
proportions to the democratic peoples of East Asia".
The fall of Mukden has also caused the Japanese
to read a new meaning into communist activities within
Japan, and considerable newspaper comment has been
directed toward increasing communist influence and
activity in the two extremities of the country, Hokkaido
and Kyushu. With both these areas closer than any other
parts of Japan to the siatic mainland, a fear is develop-
ing that Hokkaido and Kyushu are to serve as penetration
points for communist infiltration from the continent in
what may eventually become a red pincer movement against
Japan.
How concerned the Japanese have become as a result
of intensified communist activity throughout the Far
East, climaxing with the fall of Mukden, mey be discerned
from the fact that on November 2, 1948 one of the Emperor's
advisers made an unexpected call at this Mission to
in uire for any further news or appraisal of the significance
of the fall of ukden. It was obvious that the inquiry
was made on behalf of the Emperor who has previously
evinced interest in the communist problem.
Thile a cloud of deep pessimism hangs over most
Japanese, the Japanese communists are jubilant. The
conmunist daily, Akahata, in its issue of November 3
devoted an unprecedented amount of space to the red
victory in Manchuria under the headline "General Situation
Settled - Triumphant Chinese Emancipation Army". The
lengthy article was written around a hypothetical inter-
view with Mr. NoSAKA Sanzo, one of the top communist
leaders and the party's authority on Chinese affairs who
worked with the Chinese conmunists from 1940 until his
return to Japan in January, 1946.
Mr. Nosaka attributes the communist victory to the
overwhelming support the Manchurian masses have given
the "Emancipation Army" which far offsets the material
advantage the Nationalist forces have enjoyed as a result
of American armaments and financial support. Another
factor, according to Kr. Nosaka, is the superior leader-
ship of the Chinese communists as contrasted with the
incompetency and corruption of Chinese politicians and
financiers. The communist military leadership successfully
adapted its strategy to the situation while the Nationalists
blindly followed unsuitable, foreign methods. Finally
according to the article, the Chinese communist forces
based their economic and political policies on real
"democratic" principles.
Turning
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 709,
November 4, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-4-
Turning to the future, Mr. Nosaka believes that
the communist forces may soon fight decisive engagements
along the Yangtze line, and that there may be revolts
agai nst the Nationalist Government throughout other parts
of China, backed by sustained guerrilla activity.
Mr. Nosaka suggests the possibility of the emergence
of a new coalition regime for China, but only along the
lines previously proposed by Mio Tse-tung. Such a regime
will represent "the working class, farmers, intelligentsia,
and netive cupital ists", working through the Communist
Party, the Democratic League, "and other democratic
parties composed of soldiers  nd politicians". According
to .r. Nosaka, this new regime will not necessarily be
a socialist one, but a "democratic" government which will
eliminate the "semi-feudelism nd the semi-colonial
system now existing in China".
The new regime will, in r. Kosaka's opinion,
seek friendly relations with foreign nations amicably
disposed toward it, but "it vi ll be difficult to maintain
friendly relttions with foreign nations who try to inter-
fere with the democratic revolution in China and her
independence". The new Chinese regime will be pleased
to enter into friendly reletions with Japan "if and when
Japan is really democratized", and it is therefore
unfortunate, Mr. Nosaka argues, for future friendly
relations between Japan and the proposed new regime in
China "that such reactionary cabinets as the Yoshida
Cabinet came into being after the war's end".
Mr. Nosaka frankly states that Japan will be
directly affected by the communist triumph in Manchuria.
In particular, he anticipates a change in the international
position of Japan by virtue of the fact that a change
in the Chinese political scene will affect one of the
Allied Powers now ∞ ntrolling Japan.
In summary, it is not difficult to detect in Japanese
comment on the political situation on the Asiatic mainland
a latent fear that the rapid spread of comaunism in
China and elsewhere will eventually have important reper-
cussions in Japan. There is also a tendency in Japanese
comment to associate growing communist strength throughout
the Far East with the increasingly aggressive attitude
shown by the comnunists within Japan. Although the
communist position in Japan is now faced with stronger
opposition than at any time during the Occupation, it
appears likely thet the important victory the Chinese
communists have scored in Manchuria will stimulate the
Japanese communists to still greater efforts. Heretofore,
there has been some tendency in Japan to regard the
Japanese communist movement as an acelemic problem or at
most as a potential rather than an actual or immediate
danger. The attempted coup in Indonesia, the armed uprising
in South Korea and the communist capture of Mukden have
fully demonstrated the grimmer realities of communism
and there is little doubt that in the light of these
events communist activity in Japan will be viewed with
far more concern than heretofore.
Manchuria

Manchuria and Korea in particular are names all
too familiar to the Japanese people and the Japanese
are unquestionably reminding themselves today that Japan
once fought a costly war over these two territories
with the same power which now stands behind the spread
of communism in the Far East. Although the red menace
once served the propaganda of Japanese imperialism, it
is quite possible that the present situation will provide
for many Japanese a kind of historic justification of
Japan's prewar continental expansion and anti-communist
policies.

will be from Friday throush Thursday in order that the

112-948
Summary
Heaviest play in the pist week has been given to
the policy speech of Premier YOShIDA Shigeru in the Diet
and to parliamentary debate on the budget. These topics
yielded first place on only cne day to (a) the United
States Supreme Court decision to consider appeals from
the verdict of the International Military Tribunal for
the Far East, end (b) the Far Eastern Commission's pro-
posal to expand Japanese trade.
Editorial Comment on the Jar Crimes Verdict
While the United States Supreme Court decision of
December 6 to consider the appea's of ex-Jeneral DoIHARA
and ex-Premier HIROTA received gcod play as a news story
including a Tokyo Mainichi extra in the form of a handbill
it has prompted only one editorial among Tokyo newspapers

in the

class. sheet written.

-2-
in the Asahi of December 8. Asahi stated that the
Supreme Court's willingness to hear the appeal will
not fail to have great repercussions upon the Japanese
people. It warned "lest the trend of assuming a lenient
attitude toward the crimes of the Japanese war criminals
arise among the public....on the strength of criticism
of the Tokyo trial or as a result of postponing the
execution of the sentences....and the acceptance of the
appeals on the part of the Supreme Court." It added,
"We cannot help but feel that our mistaken hopes for the
postponement of the execution of sentences are rather
liable to obscure the historic mission of the trial to
establish international peace."
Asahi surmised that the Supreme Court will not under-
take to pass on the verdict of the Tribunal, but will con-
cern itself only with vhether the Tribunal was "legally
established."
The prefectural press at the end of November was
Senerally of the opinion that the Tokyo trials should be
considered closed with General Douglas MacArthur's con-
firmation of the sentences.
The Yamagata Shimbun remarked that the principal
aim of the trials was not to punish the accused, but to
make the Japanese people "reflect on their faults." The
same commentator pointed out that the punishment of the
twenty-five Class A defendants did not clear the Japanese
people of their responsibilities and that the only way
Japan could atone for "its disturbances to vorld peace
was to travel the lon, difficult road of democratic re-
construction."
In a similar vein Niigata Nippo said that in their
condemnation of the militarists, the people must not con-
sider themselves entirely blameless inasmuch as they
"obeyed and cooperated ith their leaders." Chugoku
Shimbun urged that on the day of execution, the Japanese
people "shed tears of repentance for their war sins" and
renew their determination to become a peace-loving country.
Nagasaki Minyu thought that the most important lesson to
be ained from the verdict is the necessity to exterminate
Fascism "fostered by ultre-nationalism, dictatorship and
racial superiority."
The Department will notice the difference between
these editorial opinions and the Japanese public's re-
actions to the verdict reported by Allied foreign corre-
spondents stationed here. While the foreign correspondents
in their

Tokyo's Despatch No. 775,

-3-
in their dispatches, since the verdict on November 12,
have reported the complex factors entering into the
verdict and some anticipated political repercussions,
the Japanese newspapers have assumed the duty of tutoring
their readers in the proper attitude to adopt towards the
executions.
Reactions to Premier's Policy Statement
Premier Yoshida's policy speech to the Diet (called
an "administrative speech" by the Japanese) on December 4
has drawn a volley of criticism from many quarters ranging
in political complexion from the conservative Jiji Shimpo
to the Communist Akahata.
Jiji Shimpo put the case most succinctly: "The public
Wanted to hear the Jovernment's policies at the Diet....
but except for stressing again the Government's intention
to dissolve the Diet, Premier Yoshida has not clarified any
policies worthy of note."
Sekai Keizai considered the speech meaningless, while
Mainichi described it as spineless. The latter also
pointed out that Yoshida made only two points: (1) that
after passage of the supplementary budget the House of
Representatives will be dissolved, an inter-party decision
which it says was reached at the end of the Third Diet
session, and (2) that if the Democratic Liberals are re-
turned by the voters in sufficient numbers to become the
major party, then another Yoshida cabinet will be formed.
Lainichi considered these "intra-Diet problems" and ex-
pressed its disappointment that Yoshida failed to "come
out vith a clear-cut program."
Asahi considered the policy statement so important
it devoted two editorials to it on the same day--both
adversely critical. Cne called it an "administrative
address without a policy" and the other described it as
"clumsy, inappropriate." It described the address as
"an electioneering speech rather than an administrative
speech," and compared it to "a wedding speech delivered
at the time of divorce proceedings."
Yomiuri, which generally supports the Democratic
Liberal Party, was milder in its criticism than the other
big Tokyo papers, but considered that the Premier "spoke
in too negative a tone and had no courage to appeal to
the people" with a constructive policy. Middle-of-the
road Tokyo Shimbun dismissed the statement by saying,
"We find
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 775,
December 9, 1948.
-4-
"We find nothing worthy of special mention in Premier
Yoshida's administrative speech."
The Communist organ, Akahata, developed a new
wrinkle in self-congratulation. Akahata quoted Yoshida
as havins said that in order to restore Japanese credit
abroad and conclude a peace treaty, a democratic govern-
ment must be established. Akahata continued: "It is only
the Communist Party of Japan that has consistently ad-
vocated precisely what Premier Yoshida sets forth in his
speech" and thereupon concluded that "Premier Yoshida's
words were tantamount to an adnission that a popular
government led by the Communists is the way to establish
national independence."
One of the essential factors behind Yoshida's state-
ment to the Diet received no mention in the Japanese
press. That factor is the part taken by Government Sec-
tion, Jeneral Headquarters, in the question of Diet dis-
solution and the holding of general elections.
On the resignation of Premier ASHIDA in September
considerable opinion favoring the holding of new general
elections was reported to exist among Japanese politicians.
The Government Section is generally believed to have told
Yoshida at that time that the Diet must pass the National
Public Service Law and the supplementary budget before
dissolution and new seneral elections. Economic and
Scientific section, Jeneral Headquarters, is understood
to concur in the viw thet the deficiency appropriation
budget now under consideration by the Diet must be dis-
posed of before the Diet is dissolved.
In the light of common supposition that Headquarters
relations with the Yoshida Government have been guided by
these considerations (although they have not been discussed
in the press), much of the invective thrown at Yoshida
loses point.
Reactions to the Budget
The budget presented on December 4 by Economic
Stabilization Board Director-General IZUMIYAiA has met
a generally critical reaction. The budget is characterized
by the economic daily Nihon Keizai as "nothing but a col-
lection of programs now being carried out." Nihon Keizai
believed that stabilization of the political situation
through a seneral election is necessary before a government
can devote
UNCLASSIFLED

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 775,
December 9, 1943.
-5-
can deyote itself to state administration. The same
paper said in a somewhat carping tone, "However, except-
ing that the time and method of abolishing the sales
tax and the free sale of staple foods....are under
study; that land reform will stop in the second stage;
and that labor laws will not be revised, his speech in-
cludes no items worthy of mention."
The Mainichi considered the budget speech "completely
disappointing," and "merely a lot of words." The same
newspaper chided Izumiyama for vague abstractions and
said: "He used such expressions as 'effective and funda-
mental measures will be employed' and 'overall measures
will be put into practice.' However, it is the content
of these remarks that the people want to know."
Jiji Shimpo considered that the budget speech is
an attempt to meet "political difficulties... by make-shift
measures" and that it was for the most part a mere repeti-
tion of the emergency policies which were annomced by
the Democratie Liberal Party before the fommatten of the
present cabinet.
Sekai Keizai considered that Izumiyama "rererred to
no definite economic policies. He took up the simplifica-
tion of economic controls and the abolition of unnecessary
controls, but these were already included in the program
of the former cabinet."
In this general condemnation of Premier Yoshida's
policy statement and his budget the exoessive severity
is no doubt in part a consequence of the instinctive
opposition which the Japanese press feels for politios.
Whether the press is accurately voicing the views of the
electorate is probably not ascertainable until a general
election is held.

Summary
Diet discussion of the supplementary budget anthe
public, formalized logrolling which has been incideit to
the Diet's dissolution commanded the major attention of
Tokyo papers during the week, except for two days when the
drunkenness of Finance .inister Izumiyama earned him rough
editorial scoldings. His intoxication and amorous advances
to two women Diet members allowed the newspapers to abandon
the chill intellectual aoproach to budgetary figures and
abstract arguments denouncin the unedifying political maneu-
verings of tnis Diet session. Izumiyama provided a rousing
human interest news peg on which to hang their discontent,
and the press was quick to join the avid political opposi-
tion in manifying the incident.
Behavior in the Diet
A significant feature of the editorials in the major
Tokyo newspapers discussing the Izumiyama affair was their
broadening of the responsibility for his scandalous behavi
Not only
UNCLASSIFLED
WAR 2
FILED
1
St

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 788,
December 17, 1948.
-2-
Not only was Minister Izumiyama condemned, but the Diet
in general was blamed and several editorialists insisted
that the people themselves who elected the Diet must
also recognize their share of responsibility.
Mainichi and Asahi simultaneously pointed out that
Izumiyama's drinking bout was all the more reprehensible
in that it occurred when the supplementary budget bill
was coming up for consideration. Mainichi said, "To make
matters worse, the incident took place at the very moment
when the fate of the much-debated supplementary budget,
which would surely affect the welfare of all public em-
ployees and their families, amounting to 10,000,000 people,
was about to be decided." Asahi pointed out that the
budget bill in hand dealt with the new wage standard which
"directly concerns the treatment of Government and public
office workers...."
The extreme tone assumed by the press over the in-
cident is a further illustration of Japanese sensitivity
to matters of dignity in precedence to matters of political
substance. sahi said: "This is the ugliest and most in-
decent incident in the whole history of Japanese parlia-
mentary politics." Jiji Shimpo went even further, calling
it "an event unprecedented in the history of the world."
Mainichi and Yomiuri both considered that parliamentary
procedures are imperiled. Hainichi said, "When after the
war the Diet was elevated by the new constitution to the
status of the highest organ of the state, the people hoped
that its enhanced dignity would put an end to such dis-
reputable acts. This hope, however, has been betrayed com-
pletely." Yomiuri concurred in this attitude and said,
"This is the saddest news ever heard of the Diet."
In similar vein, Tokyo Shimbun recalled that the
Diet is the supreme organ of state power and said, "How-
ever, the actual Diet is a place of ignoble political
warfare and Diet members are far from being the ideal of
the people." Tnis ne spaper went on to say that since
"most" of the Diet members are suspected of implication
in the electric, textile, coal mine control and other
scandals, "it is no wonder that the Izumiyama incident
occurred in the Diet. It is no exaggeration to say that
the incident is a symbol of the present Diet."
Mainichi felt that "If this state of things con-
tinues any longer, we shall inevitably lose the confidence
of the orld, as well as the opportunity for national
independence." Yomiuri, in somber mood, found that "The
Japanese Diet which earlier invited worid-wide misgivings
by producins many crime suspects, including the former
premier

December 17, 1948.
-3-
premier in relation to the Showa electrical scandal,
has now produced an unprecedented incident of drunken-
ness." Jiji Shimpo considered that "the news is also
so scandalous that, if reported to foreign countries,
it will undoubtedly compromise faith in the Japanese
Government."
The responsibility of the people for having elected
their representatives was emphasized by Tokyo Shimbun,
which asked the people to "reflect upon our own responsi-
bilities for having chosen such slovenly Diet members
as our representatives." Mainichi considered that the
incident Was "a reflection of the lack of morality of
the people at large," while isahi drew an interesting
psychological parallel between the misbehavior on the
Diet floor and "the behavior of the Jananese during the
war, which croused severe criticism throughout the world."
Inukai lection
The election of IMulI Men to the presidency of
the Democratic Party as successor to ex-Premier ASHIDA
received good play at tihe beginning of the week. Asahi
prominently played his inaugural speech under the heading,
"Inukai Advocates Sound Reform Folicies for Democrats"
and mentioned his commitment to "revised capitalism."
Mainichi discussed his election in the light of a possible
merger with other conervative forces in the Liberal Demo-
cratic Party headed by Premier YOSnIDA.
Prefectural Hewspapers
The prefectural press (i.e., newspapere published
outside the Tokyo metropolitan area) at the beginning of
December save strongest emphasis to the necessity for
purifyins politics. The arrest of ex-Premier Ashida in
connection With the howa lectric scandal led to broad
demands for a thorou h clean-up. In similar editorials,
Toyama shimbun and Csaka Shimbun pointed out that never
before in Japanese history had a person as prominent as a
Prime Minister been charged with a crime by the public
prosecutors. These two nevispapers felt that, besides
undangering Japanese credit abroad, the arrest of Ashida
had undermined the people's faith in politics. Kyoto
Shimbun also made the latter point. Hiigata Nippo con-
sidered that the scandals were due to several factors
one of
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 788,
December 17, 1948.
-4-
one of which is the bureaucratic system itself. Exem-
plifying the Japanese tendency to emphasize collective
responsibility, the Shinano Mainichi considered that in
the Ashida case the whole Diet "should bear some of the
responsibility for the dishonor."
War Crimes Verdict Discussed in Magazines
While Japanese magazines in recent months have
given very little space to the findings of the secondary
war crimes trials, the verdict of the International
Military Tribunal for the Far East on Class A war criminals
has prompted considerable coverage. Many writers stressed
that the verdict is a judgment ajainst all Japanese. The
significance of the judgment to interna tional law and the
Tribunal's objective of outlawin aggressive warfare were
stressed and the most frequently quoted statement was
that of Chief Prosecutor Keenan to the effect that the
Tribunal's objective was not retaliation but the prevention
of war.
Description in the judgment of many of the Japanese
war atrocities apparently made a considerable impression
on numerous writers. For ins tance, in Asahi Hyoron one
writer said, "What struck my conscience most while I
listened to the verdict was the cruelty exhibited by the
Japanese Army. I cannot tell how many decades it will
take us to wipe away this blot. I could not bear to
listen to it, but we must hear it." In Horitsu No Hiroba
another writer stated, "Although it is distressing to
look back upon our defeat, it is even more painful to
look back upon the atrocities by Class A, B and C war
criminals." Almost all magazine writers on war crimes
trials urged that the Japanese people must learn the
lessons implicit in the trials.
The jurisprudence involved in the war crimes trials
has been critically examined by at least two magazines.
In Choryu, Tokyo University Professor UCHIDA Rikizo found
that the Tribunal sought to enlarge the body of inter-
national law by bringing three types of charges: crimes
against the peace, crimes in contravention of the rules
of warfare, and crimes against humanity. He found that
while no question could arise concerning the second of
these, the re was no precedent for the first and third.
Hence, he concluded that punishment meted out would run
counter to the British and merican th eory of jurisprudence
which condemns ex post facto law. Professor Uchida con-
cluded that "there may exist some question as to the juris-
prudential authority of the Tribunal. But I suppose this
is unavoidable in the realization of the great and just
objectives."
UNCLASSIFLED

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.97)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 788,
December 17, 1948.
-5-
objeetives."
In Chunichi ieekly one writer found the judgment
"a notable one, worthy of a major and historical trial.
Particularly it renders an advanced and positive decision
on the facts of aggression." But he complained that only
fifteen of the l2l3 pages of the verdict are devoted to a
discussion of the jurisprudence involved. Professor
YOKOTA Kisaburo writing in Chuo Koron found the trials
"historic" in that they have brought to an end "the old
Japan" and compelled the ma jor criminals to "bear the
responsi bility for their successive acts of aggression
and war." He stated that by providing a precedent, the
trials "will serve as a deterrent to future attempts at
war."

play in the Eastern Hemisphere."

Rice-Flanting Quotas
Assignaent of the 1949 quotas for rice and other staple foods
announced on Dece.nber 22 at a veeting of prefectural governors in
Tokyo overshadowed all other news for two days, but prompted only
rour editorials in major newspapers in the next two davs, namely:
Asahi, Nihon Keizai, Jiji Shimpo and Sekai K izai. Three of these
newspapers pointed out that the CAr directive providing for pre-
planting quotas, hich could be increased should the harvest justify
any change, is a departure fron the practice followed in the past
year. Nihon heizai put it nost succinctly hen it said, "GHQ announced
that it had instructed the Japanese Government to revise the laws in
such a manner as to make possible additional rice delivery quotas.
When this year's pre-allocatio: (pre-planted allocation) was deter
.ined in ..arch, it was decided that adaitional delivery allotments
would not be made."
Discussing farmers' possible reactions, ihon Keizai said,
"I armers may consider the new pre-allotments as being too harsh"
and came to the nub of the natter when it said, "When the taxes on
farmers' revenue are fixed next January, the agrarian economy will
becone further straigntened. such inancial stringency in agrarian
districts is due....to h: fact that farn products are priced lower
than production mat rials and daily necessities....The problem is
the price of rice." wihon reizai here puts its finger on the problem
which has been worrying observers in many fields--namely, that while
the price of rice is fixed, the prices of tools, clothes and con-
struction materials have been steadily swelling in Japan's infla-
tionary bulge.
Asahi considered tiat stricer collection of foods "will weigh
heavily upon the farmers," but urged then to realize that this was
necessary in view of "wage and rice control hich constitutes the
foundation of the program of econo.aic independence." Asahi then
came to the somewhat surprising conclusion that to "maximize" agri-
cultural production, "the Government and the farmers must endeavor
to reform the prevailing system of small-scale farming."
There is an overtone of fear among editorials scanned that the
new aystem of food collection, including increased quotas at harvest
time, will be badly received by Japanese farmers and possibly lead to
refusals to sow the grain.
In the Asahi, Nihon Keizai and Jiji Shimpo editorials these
pnrases occur:

food....

DEPARIMENT OF STATE
Subject: Japanese Newspaper Reactions for the Week Ending
January 6, 1949.
PM
3
0
Honorable
R
894.
670l
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch No.
11/
818 of December 31, 1948 on the above subject and to transmit
a summary of Japanese newspaper reactions to current events
for the week of December 31, 1948 through January 6, 1949.
-749
Summary
Foreign affairs received the principal news play in the
week under review, the favorite topics being the course of the
Chinese crisis, the convening of the Eighty-First Congress in
Washington and President Truman's speech on the state of the
A/MJS
nation, and the possibility of the reparations policy for Japan
being revised. Local stories revolved mainly around the local
elections.
Year-End Reviews
FEB 4 1949
Three editorials -- in Asahi, Nihon Keizai and Tokyo Shimbun
-- reviewed the accomplishments of 1948 in a spirit of confidence
FILED
and optimism. Asahi considered that Japan has experienced no
period so full of change as the past three and a half years since
the surrender, called the policies of the Far Eastern Commission
and the Allied Occupation "unusually successful," and regretted
"that we cannot predict the time of the peace treaty.... Apart
from this, the solution of various questions rests with the
Japanese themselves." Asahi considered that the two questions
of first importance are "to grasp the true spirit and not to
imitate the mere forms of democracy" and to "tackle the problem
of economic reconstruction," realizing that this concerns the
Japanese themselves.
Nihon
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 45 === (confidence 1.00)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 10,
January 7, 1949.
-2
Nihon Keizai even more optimistically claimed that Japanese
"society as a whole has been shifting from the abnormal to the
normal during the past year. This does not mean a return to the
past nor a revival of the old customs. It means that the political,
economic and social distortions have been corrected and that society
as a whole has been stabilized to a certain degree by retaining
what was worth retaining from the past and casting aside what is
inappropriate.... There are bright prospects ahead of us."
In somewhat similar vein, Tokyo Shimbun claimed that during
1948 "as the confusion abated, extremes and shams were severely
criticized and repudiated; the people began to discriminate between
the genuine and the false. In this sense, the year 1948 was the
turning point of postwar Japan."
SCAP's New Year's Message
General MacArthur's New Year's message captured the lead spot
in all dailies on January 1, 1949, except in the communist organ,
Akahata. The editorials prompted by that message in the succeeding
days concerned themselves principally with the restoration to the
Japanese people of the unrestricted use and display of the Japanese
flag. Students of things Japanese are generally agreed that the
Japanese attitude to the flag is not identical with the respect
which Nestern people give it. The Japanese reserve for the Imperial
portrait, and possibly for the Imperial crest, that measure of
reverence which in the West is given to even a tattered bit of
cloth. In view of this difference of approach to the symbolism
of the flag, it is perhaps not surprising that Tokyo Shimbun and
Asahi both called for distribution of new flags to the Japanese
people. The first of these newspapers pointed out that "the majority
of the Japanese people have lost their flags or have turned them
over to other uses. It is our sincere hope that the Government
will take steps to distribute national flags to the people at low
cost." Asahi stated that "although the national flag has been freed,
the people have none in their possession....the Government should
ration real flags to the people."
Elections
The Japanese press has been following the early development
of the election campaign with a statistical as well as an analytical
approach. Several newspapers, outstanding among which is Mainichi,
have been closely tabulating the number of candidates filing for
election. Candidates, according to Mainichi. as of January 5, 1949,
totaled 1357, including 452 Democratic Liberals, 209 Democrats and
188 Socialists. Communist Party Headquarters were quoted by Nihon
Keizai as announcing that the conmunist official slate totaled ll6.
The analytical approach to the elections is still in general terms.
Asahi pointed out that the majority of candidates are new men and
that this fact makes the election significant, and called on "old-
fashioned
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 46 === (confidence 1.00)
Tokyo's Despatch No, 10,
January 7, 1949.
-3-
fashioned candidates" to "retire and give their places to new
candidates. The coming general election must mean the airing
of the House of Representatives."
Yomiuri returned to its tilting against the multiplication
of small parties. This powerful Tokyo daily, whose conservative
slant is more pronounced than either Asahi or Mainichi, stated,
"for Japan's reconstruction the political situation must first be
stabilized, and to accomplish this a strong government must be
formed." Yomiuri therefore considered that "the confusion of a
large number of small parties vying for power must be avoided as
much as possible and a direct advance must be made toward the
ideal of bipartisan politics" (this Mission's despatch under ref-
erence, page 3). In substantiation of its dislike of small parties,
it pointed out that "very small parties are the ones that are
naking lush promises" and therefore "it is sheer folly to accept
the promises of small parties at their face value."
Yomiuri also returned to the charge against the fair-weather
advocates of economic stabilization (this Mission's despatch under
reference, pages 3 and 4). It warned that "some candidates empha-
size that the nine-point program (the United States interim directive
on economic stabilization) is identical with the policy followed
by their parties for years, while others say that it is nothing
but an effort by capital to colonize Japan. These conflicting
claims are, to our regret, being made everywhere by irresponsible
speakers. They only make it more difficult for the general public
to make the right choice." Yomiuri then demanded that constituents
distinguish between truth and falsehood in such speeches.
China
Successive steps in the development of the China crisis have
received editorial attention as thev arose and Generalissimo CHIANG
Kai-shek's New Year's announcement that he would accept "honorable
peace" prompted editorials in several newspapers. Jiji Shimpo, for
instance, considered that the chances for peace in China are slight.,
feeling "with regret that the communists are too imperious to accept
any overtures on the part of the government" and saw "but little hope
for peace in the near future." Mainichi felt that Chiang's message
opened a new phase in the progress of the civil war, but that even
if the Generalissimo refused to resign now, his "retirement....will
come to the fore again in the near future."
To Nihon Keizai the question of China is bound up with world
peace and two paragraphs of an editorial of January 5, 1949 are
quoted, since they are believed to express succinctly a widespread
Japanese attitude of mind:
"The question of China, however, looms as a big issue. If the
communists control the central plains of China, the whole of
Asia will be influenced. Even though the efforts to consolidate
anti-communist influence in Europe succeed, they ma fail in
Asia, and the world balance between the Soviet Union and the
United States may be upset.
"As for
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 10,
January 7, 1949.
-4-
"As for Japan, thanks to the aid of the Allied countries, Japan's
economic recovery is slowly but surely progressing. With the
shift in the balance of power in the Orient as a result of the
advances of the Chinese communists in the central plains of Asia,
Japan's position is being reconsidered. This occasion provides
an excellent opportunity for the Japanese people to regain inter
national confidence and to return to international society as a
propelling force for peace. Every member of the Japanese nation
must put forth his best efforts to rebuild the motherland and
overcome all immediate difficulties."
The Farm Problem
Japanese farming received attention in Asahi and Mainichi. The
former contrasted "bright and gloomy villages" in a long editorial,
finding that the "bright" villages are those in which land reform
has been properly carried out, whereas the "gloomy" villages are those
where "land reforms were carried out by landowners and upper-bracket
farmers." It found that in the "bright" villages the trend is towards
cooperation and collaboration with villagers "brimming with vitality."
On the other hand, it considered that in the "gloomy" villages "the
spirit of selfish personal profit predominates and there are no signs
of cooperation between farmers."
Mainichi approached the question of crop collection quotas in un-
easy fashion, though it did not voice its fears openly as did some of
its colleagues (this Mission's despatch under reference, page 5 et seq.).
Mainichi foresaw that United States assistance in the form of food "is
scheduled for gradual decrease" and for a remedy could only suggest
further "self-sacrifice" on the part of the farmers. Mainichi's too
facile argument, with its chill comfort for the farmers themselves,
was as follows: "The only alternative will be to increase the farmers'
food delivery. The nation must rely upon the farmers' endeavors.
Farmers are the first victims of inflation, and yet the nation must
demand a greater sacrifice of them. As the saying goes, farming is
the foundation of the country. The nation depends upon the farmers'
self-sacrifices for its survival. Farming communities may be crushed
under national pressure, but this must be avoided by government policies."
The newspaper then argued that land should be redistributed in a more
efficient fashion.
Mainichi carried a story about two slave brokers in northern
Honshu under investigation. The newspaper quoted labor standards
officials as saying that these brokers had sold "a large number of
persons, mostly young boys and girls," into forced labor.
Allied Council Meeting
Top news play in Tokyo newspapers was given to the meeting on
January 5, 1949 of the Allied Council for Japan, when the Japanese
police were discussed. Editorial comment had not time to appear
within the period under review.
Respectfully yours,
631.1
W.J. Sebald

Significant newspaper reactions for week ending January 13
were as follows:
1. Contemplated revisions in labor laws were regarded as
having been made necessary by lack of self-restraint on part
of unions.
2. Discussion of Japanese police at Allied Council on
January 5 prompted one editorial advocating improvement in
894.504
εelection and training of police.
3. Comments on elections ran to exhortations to turn

which makes for a lot of small parties. The Nippon Times
sharply attacked the electoral law which, though intended
to guard against political corruption, allegedly had given
894.0
every indication of favoring political machines and older
politicians.
4. Yoshida's intimation that government is contemplating
establishment of a purge appeal committee for primary purpose,
as so reported, to release village and town chiefs purgees and
those purgees who had valuable foreign trade contacts, was
denied by GHQ on January 12.
7
5. A good deal of concern was reflected in most papers
over the complications arising from the fixing of the rice
price at a rate which was regarded as squeezing the farmer
seriously between low prices and high operating costs. No
editorials criticized the etabilization directive calling

Summary
Foreign news dispatches received more emphasis during the week
under review than local news, although much of the press deplored
public apathy toward the elections now only ten days away. Principal
local stories drawing editorial comment were the proposed revision
of two basic labor laws and intricate speculations concerning the
establishment of a single yen exchange rate.
Labor Law Revisions
The intention of the Government to present to the new House of
Representatives to be elected January 23, 1949 amendments to the
Labor Union Law and the Labor Relations Adjustment Law briefly cap-
tured lead positions. Subsequent editorial comment in the metropolitan
Tokyo press, as exemplified by Mainichi and Asahi. emphasized that
these revisions are not intended to deprive labor of its rights to
collective bargaining, but have been made necessary by the lack of
self-restraint and the indiscriminate strike tactics of organized
labor in the past year. Mainichi considered that "the imperative
need to place labor-capital relations upon a healthy foundation had
long been felt in all quarters," and since the proposed legislative
amendments did not deprive labor of collective bargaining nor of the
right to
UNCLASSIFIED
lass shict written

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 22,
January 14, 1949.
-2
right to strike, the "contents of the revision bill include no
points which should be opposed when viewed in the light of the
economic situation in Japan today." Asahi blamed labor for the
proposed amendments. "We sincerely regret," it stated, "that the
proposed revision of labor legislation could not have been avoided
by unionists' self-awakening and self-restraint...We agree with
some unionists who assert that the Government and the capitalists
were negligent. They should remember that their strike ideology
has greatly retarded reconstruction." A welcome clarification of
the Government's intentions was furnished by Labor Minister MASUDA,
who stated that the Government did not intend to revise the Labor
Standards Law.
Allied Council Meeting
The meeting of the Allied Council for Japan on January 5, 1949,
when the Soviet Member brought up for discussion the organization of
the Japanese police, prompted Jiji Shimpo to comment that "the mere
hearing of rearming and waging an aggressive war makes the Japanese
people tremble. To associate an increase in police strength with
rearming is indeed a surprise to the Japanese." It admitted that the
police are at present unable to keep peace and order, but suggested
that bettering the police lay in inproving the selection and training
of policemen, giving them pride in their work and raising their
salaries.
New Secretary of State
The resignation of General MARSHALL as Secretary of State re-
ceived top news play and prompted editorials in Yomiuri and Mainichi
in which both joined in recounting his achievements while in office.
The appointment of Mr. Dean ACHESON to succeed General Marshall was
hailed in both editorials as a development of world-wide importance.
Mainichi recalled that Mr. Acheson, when about to resign as Under-
secretary, had stressed the need of rebuilding Germany and Japan into
workshops for the continents of Lurope and Asia. Both newspapers
concurred that the change in Secretaries of State would bring about
no drastic change in United States foreign policy. Yomiuri, however,
stated: "The new Secretary of State, being a civilian, will introduce
a new flavor into America's foreign policy."
Elections
The quantity of news play and editorial comment on the elections
has been considerable. The recurrent theme of editorials was to
encourage
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 22,
January 14, 1949.
-3-
encourage public interest. "Have interest in the elections!" Yomiuri
cried, and several newspapers made the point that political rallies
are poorly attended. Yomiuri advised the voters to "vote for parties
rather than individuals," a position taken by a number of other papers.
Mainichi considered that "it is foolish to evaluate candidates aside
from the parties to which they belong." Tokyo Times (Japanese-language)
considered that "history proves that over-emphasis on the individual
results in a number of small parties. Tokyo Shimbun considered that
voting for the candidate as distinguished from the party is dangerous
for democratic government, if the tendency should develop into a public
denial of political parties as a whole.
The English-language Nippon Times stated in an unusually strong
editorial that the electoral law, which was designed to guard against
political corruption, has paradoxically given every indication of
favoring the well-established, older politicians. The Nippon Times
said:
"Under the restrictions imposed by these rules, the use of posters
and handbills is virtually prohibited; the sending of campaign
literature through the mails is severely limited; house-to-house
canvassing is forbidden; and speech-making is limited to a very
few meetings held under official auspices except for impromptu
street-corner soap-box rallies where the candidate must be present
in person. Except for the impromptu street meetings, expenses for
virtually all permissible campaign activities are to be defrayed
from the public treasury."
"It is an open secret," this newspaper stated, "that there is
wholesale resort to surreptitious illegal campaigning and, being carried
on under risk of arrest, this black market campaigning is more expensive!..
Even here, Nippon Times found that the experienced, old-time, reactionary
politicians have the advantage due to their smoothly functioning machines,
while new candidates are likely to be clumsy in their illegal campaigning
and easily detected by the police.
Considerable news play was given to the seizure of an extra issue
by the Communist Party organ, Akahata, as a violation of the Electoral
Law.
Revision of Purge Directive
On January 10, 1949 Premier YOSHIDA revealed that the Government
is considering the establishment of a committee of appeal for the re-
screening of certain categories of purged Japanese officials. He was
quoted in Mainichi as follows: "The Government wants to release im-
mediately from the purge those who held positions of village and town
chiefs during the war and who are capable of contributing to a sound,
healthy growth of local government." Apparently as an afterthought,
he was
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 52 === (confidence 1.00)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 22,
January 14, 1949.
-4
he was also quoted as wanting to "unpurge" a number of persons in
order that "if and when foreign trade among private traders is boosted,
the services of men who enjoy the confidence of foreigners" will be
available. Inasmuch as an official of Government Section, General
Headquarters, denied on January 12 any moves to permit the re-screening
of purged categories, Premier Yoshida's trial balloon was punctured
before it had time to get under way. Dai Ichi Shimbun, a general-appeal
newspaper of small circulation and middle-of-the-road conservatism,
though distinctly critical of the Democratic Liberal Party and Premier
Yoshida, flatly considered Premier Yoshida's statement as an electioneer
ing stunt and commented, "Although this (re-screening) is not yet a
problem worthy of special notice, caution should be exercised against
the tricky intentions behind the conservative reactionary forces."
Crop Quotas in the Prefectural Press
The tone of the prefectural press editorials discussing the 1949
rice quotas and the agricultural problem in general was one of accept-
ance of an unpleasant situation. More and more the press is reflect-
ing a consciousness of aggravating difficulties in rural areas, which
apparently sense the complications deriving from Japan's high-cost
agriculture. None of eight editorials scanned, however, took a position
other than that the United States nine-point interim directive calling
for increased food production was warranted. When mentioning the matter,
all tried to justify the assignment of additional rice delivery quotas
when actual harvests exceed pre-planting estimates.
Using sharp language, Sangyo heisai Shimbun found that farmers
are caught in a "squeeze" between low prices for agricultural produce
and high prices for industrial products and feared that "although the
agricultural panic due to overproduction and cheap foreign foodstuffs
is not at hand, the signs of a panic are undeniably becoming apparent."
This newspaper chided labor for "the laborers' policy of resorting to
strikes only for increased wages at the sacrifice of production" and
scolded industry for its "stagnation of production." It also pointed
out that, "while agricultural production has almost regained the prewar
level, manufacturing industries are still unable to produce more than
fifty per cent of the prewar level." It then demanded that the authori-
ties "adopt rigid policies toward mining and industrial enterprises be-
cause increased supply of staple foods has not always been accompanied
by increased production in these fields of industry."
Niigata Nippo commented that Niigata Prefecture's rice delivery
quota has been increased by 1.5%, "which is above the average for the
whole country," and advised all Japanese to remember two points: (1)
that Japanese must exert themselves to the utmost to rebuild their
country before depending on foreign assistance, and (2) food being
in short supply in Japan, "we should have gained the confidence of
foreign countries by making prompt deliveries above quotas before we
were pressed for them." Together with Shin Ehime Shimbun and Too Nippo,
this newspaper
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 53 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 22,
January 14, 1949.
-5-
this newspaper considered that SCAP's nine-point directive on
economic stabilization paved the way for the reconstruction of
Japan and its independence. Too Nippo also stated that the maximum
possible collection is an "absolute pre-condition for the importation
of supplementary quantities of food.
Akita Sakigake pointed out that pre-planting estimates were
hitherto only revised downwards in consequence of crop destructions
by flood or other disasters and that the revision upwards when good
harvests occur is a reasonable amendment to the crop delivery quota
system. San-In Nichinichi Shimbun considered that the key to the
delivery problem is whether the quotas are fairly allotted to the
individual farmer.
Dr. Arthur RAPER's Press Conference.
A press conference held in Tokyo on January 10, 1949 by Dr.
Arthur RAPER, Consultant, Agricultural Division, Natural Resources
Section, General Headquarters, in which he discussed his findings
on revisiting a group of thirteen farm villages after an interval
of eighteen months, was surprisingly lightly covered by the Japanese
press. While Doctor Raper was generally optimistic, he referred to
the farmers' "vague fears of an agricultural depression," and was
quoted by International News Service in the Nippon Times as stating
that the farmers were'hot necessarily wrong in their fears."

Subject: Japanese Newspaper Reactions for Week Ending

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch
no. 22 of January 14, 1949 and to transmit a sunmary of Japanese
newspaper reactions to current events for the week of January 14
through January 20, 1949.
Summary
Against the background of a continuing current of news and
editorial att ention devot ed to the general elections scheduled
for January 23, top news play during the week under review has
frequently been given to developments abroad having a direct
bearing on Japanese affairs. The announcement of a new policy
governing foreign investments in Japan, the China situation,
and the impending visit to the Far East of Secretary of the Army
Kenneth C. Royall have all in turn taken the news spotlight.
The controversy over hether the press can support and oppose
individual candidates, which captured considerable local coverage,
was discussed in this Mission's despatch no. 38 of January 20, 1949.

Tokyo, February 4, 1949.

UNCLASSIFIED (For Department use only)
Subject: Japanese Newspaper Reactions for Week Ending February 3,
1949
OPY
1les
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
894.9111/
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch no. 58
of January 28, 1949 and to previous despatches on the above sub-
ject, and to transmit a summary of Japanese newspaper reactions
to current events for the week of January 28 through February 3,
1949.
Summary
2-449
Editorial attention has been devoted principally to the
Labor Management Conference held in Tokyo last week and the visit
to the Far East of Secretary of the Army Kenneth C. ROYALL, with
brief attention to the STALIN peace offer and the YOSHIDA Govern-
ment's plans to appoint a seven-man Public Officials Re-screening
Committee.
Stalin Peace Offensive
The International News Service dispatch containing Premier
Stalin's contributions to the Soviet peace offensive was given
excellent news play throughout the metropolitan press, but edi-
torial comment has been confined to two papers, Dai Ichi Shimbun
and Nihon Keizai. The former considered that "new developments
in American foreign policy singe the inauguration of President
TRUMAN, the appointment of Secretary of State ACHESON and the
rumor about the resignation of General CLAY tend to strengthen
this hope (for peace)." Nihon Keizai linked the tentative send-
ing of Chief Justice VINSON to Moscow with the appointment of
12
Secretary
UNCLASSIFIED
873
1945
5

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 71,,
February 4, 1949.
-2
Secretary of State Acheson as indicating American wishes for peace,
but considered that Premier Stalin's statement to INS was aimed at
settling unrest in Russia, preventing the conclusion of the North
Atlantic Security Pact and impeding the rearmament of the United
States. "The chief cause of difficulty lies in Russia's basic
policy -- bolshevization of the entire world," Nihon Keisai said.
"As long as this point remains unsettled, peace declarations are
hardly significant."
Labor-Management Conference
The conferences among representatives of labor and management
held in Tokyo on January 27 and 28, 1949, to promote labor-managemet
cooperation to fulfill the economic stabilization directives prompted
considerable editorial comment, the general tone of which was that
the Japanese economy can be stabilized if all interested parties do
their share. Sekai Keizai considered as a prerequisite "putting the
relationship between labor and management on a scientific and rational
basis, to eliminate their motional differences and to realize the
fair distribution of profits in a peaceful way." Nihon Keizai called
attention to the statement which General W.F. MARQUAT, Chief of
Economic and Scientific Section, General Headquarters, made in his
address at the opening or the conference to the effect that United
States aid would be available only in proportion to the efforts of
the Japanese people themselves, and commented: "What is demanded of
labor and management at present is not discussion whether production
can be increased but exertion of every effort to hike output with the
conviction that they can do t." This newspaper counseled that should
labor and management decline to cooperate, "strong measures should be
taken to secure the necessary cooperation." Jiji Shimpo considered
that "Japan has reached a stage wherein labor and management must
unite to bring about the nation's economic independence in line with
the nine-point Economic Stabilization Program." Asahi emphasized
"the principle of the equality of burdens" to be borne by labor and
management in carrying out the Economic Stabilization Program. Asahi
also noted that since issuance of the United States interim directive
the basic policies concerning Japanese recovery had changed slightly
in direction, and stated that instead of the United States assisting
Japanese economy to recover, "the Economic Recovery Program has shifted
to that of revival by self-help." Mainichi considered it to be "highly
desirable that both labor and capital endeavor to settle (their disputes)
from an intelligent and cooperative standpoint instead of resorting to
force or being obsessed with ideologies such as 'labor aggression' or
'capital aggression.'"
In a
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 71,
February 4, 1949.
-3
In a second editorial after the conferences had closed,
Nihon Keizai noted that the principle that wages be stabilised
at present levels was adopted. It pointed out, however, that
the questions of revision of the labor laws and care for the
unemployed were left unsettled by the conference, together with
"industrial rationalization." (This latter phrase is used to
mean industrial efficiency)
Yomiuri approached its discussion of the Labor Management
Conference from a alightly different angle and took the oppor
tunity to warn that the labor movement in Japan is at a crossroads.
Yomiuri pointed out that in the history of Japan's labor movement
the term "labor-management cooperation" has long been looked on
with suspicion, though, it added, "without any definite, logical
basis." It said: "Under the current circumstanees, emphasis must
be placed on the national point of view rather than on the class
viewpoint. To raise the nation's economy to a level of self-
sufficieney must be the supreme objective for all Japmese." To
that end Yomiuri considered "the first requisite to the reconstrue-
tion of union activities is to eradicate the formalistic idea that
unions are a medium of struggle." This newspaper considered it
to be "self-evident that if one wishes to develop the enterprise
to which one belongs he must cooperate with the operator. This
problem has nothing to do with political ideologies."
Discussion of Communist-Socialist Merger
The Socialist Party's refusal of the Communist Party's formal
invitation to join it received wide news coverage but inconsiderable
editorial comment, as though the socialist position had been fore-
seen and naturally expected. Continuing the previously noted
solicitude for the future of the Socialist Party (this Mission's
despatch under reference, page two), Yomiuri advised the socialists
to expand their appeal "to seek the support of a large class of
workers out side of organized labor and tenant farmers." Yomiuri
considered that if the Socialist Party would rid itself of "squeam-
ishness at being called the third bourgeois party," it could perhaps
merge with a part of the Democratic Party and of the People's
Cooperative Party "to form a completely new organization." In some-
what similar tone, Tokyo Nichi Nichi (affiliated with Mainichi)
advised the Socialist Party to make its comeback by claiming to be
social reformers.
Purge Appeal Committee
The Yoshida Government's bland moves to create a Public Official
Re-screening Committee have been reported step by step, but the
only
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.98)
eeS AM
Tokyo's Despatch No. 71,
February 4, 1949.
-4-
only editorial on the subject which has come to the attention of
this Mission was published in Jiii Shimpo. This newspaper approved
the idea of such a new committee to which purged persons could
appeal for re-screening, but warned that "neither abolition nor
relaxation of the present purge directive is thinkable." Jiji Shimpo
considered that as a result of the 'mechanical application of the
purge directive we shall naturally look forward to the resumption of
the Appeal Committ ee."
Secretary Rovall's Visit
The visit to Japan and the Far East of Secretary of the Army
Kenneth C. Royall has released a flood of speculative oditorials
most of which are mutually repetitive. Secretary Royall's importance
in the national defense organization of the United States, the fact
that he is accompanied by military, financial and economic experts,
and that his visit coincides with present efforts to make plans for
the economic stabilization of Japan, including the establishment
of the yen-dollar exchange rate, have all been noted. The presence
of Lieutenant General Albert C. WEDEMEYER in Secretary Royall's party
was connected by Asahi with the "overwhelming victory of commmist
forces in China," which this newspaper considered "as presenting
important problems to the United States concerning her policy toward
Asia." Mainichi came to the conclusion in its speculations that
"Secretary Royall will assuredly find reason to hope for the inde-
pendence of Japan's economy and to pin hopes on Japan as a bulwark
against any new form of totalitarianism." Hochi Shimbun noted that
Secretary Royall's visit was announced shortly after issuance of
the United States interim directive calling for economic stabilisation
of Japan. This newspaper considered that the second most important
meaning of the mission concerns military strategy in the Far East,
thus accounting for the presence of Lieutenant General Wedemeyer in
Secretary Royall's party. Sekai Keizai considered tht "with Secretary
Royall's visit to this country Japanese economy is going to make
great strides."
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

=== Page 74 === (confidence 1.00)
FOREWORD
The Japanese language is universally acknowledged as being unique and at the
same time an extremely difficult language. This has been one of the greatest deterents
to foreigners in their attempts to gain a full insight into conditions existing in Japan
as well as in gaining an understanding of the way of thinking of the Japanese people,
hindering to no small extent the establishment of international harmony between
Japan and the outside world. The fault lies to an extent with the Japanese people
themselves. They should have striven in a more positive manner to broaden and
deepen international understanding, but, sad to say, their efforts to date have fallen
short of the mark. The fact that the Japan Newspaper Publishers' and Editors' Asso-
ciation has taken upon itself to publish in the English language an outline of the past
and present of the newspaper world in this country lies in no other reason than to fur-
ther international understanding towards Japanese journalism.
We feel safe in saying that journali ;m in Japan, of all Japanese institutions, has
always retained the most democratic features of the country. The history of Japanese
journalistic effort is the long history of a struggle against the various influences that
have retarded the growth of democracy in Japan and of a bitter fight to gain com-
plete freedom of speech against the clancliques of the Meiji Era, government bureau-
cracy and against the military oligarchs. Particularly, during the Taisho Era, when
liberalism made some headway in Japan, Japanese newspapers through its news and
editorial columns succeeded in becoming a dominant social influence, backed as it was
by public opinion yet at the same time standing at the vanguard of public opinion.
However, the so-called Manchurian Incident marked the beginning of ever increasing
suppressive measures against the press on the part of the militarists, until finally, the
pen was forced to succumb to the sword and watch in helplessness the spectacle of
Japan being dragged into the tragedy of the Pacific War.
Only through the crushing defeat was Japan able to re-plant her feet on the
path towards democratic development. The newspaper world also rose from the ashes
of war, broke asunder its shackles and resumed its march towards the complete free-
dom of the press. Today, outwardly, the Japanese press give the impression that it
has retrogressed to a stage of development such as existed at least 20 to 30 years ago.
These surface signs are due to the limitations that have been imposed upon the Japa-
nese press with reference to space and dead-line, factors which stem from the current
shortage of newsprint and other materials and the general unfavorable conditions of
productive activity in Japan. But, in a moral sense the Japanese press has made
marked progress. Newspapermen, first and foremost, as well as the general public have
come to understand the gravity of the mission of the press in a democratic society. A
"free press" is about to take root in the native soil of Japan.
In presenting this book to the general public, we wish to announce that it is
our plan to continue the regular publication of this handbook in the future. It is
6ur sincere hope that from the next edition the publication will show marked improve
ment to become an organized. and regular report on the growth of the "free press" in
Japan.
MASANORI ITO,
President,
Japan Newspaper Publishers'
and Editors' Association

Nippon
Times
The Oldest and the Most Reliable English-Language
Newspaper in Japan
Through its pages, the journal strives accurately to
mirror the political, economic, cultural and social aspects
of the nation which is in the midst of the throes of rebirth.
To understand and appreciate Japan under the new
Constitution, its role, aspiration and immediate and ulti-
mate goals as a Democracy, the NIPPON TIMES is the
most suitable medium.
Its foreign news coverage is up-to-date and com-
plete, made possible through arrangements with the
world's greatest news sources: United Press, Associated
Press, International News Service, Reuters, Agence
France Presse, New York Herald Tribune, King Features
Syndicate, Kyodo, Radiopress and Sun-Acme.
NIPPON TIMES, LTD.
1, 1-chome, Uchisaiwai-Cho, Chiyoda-ku,
TOKYO, JAPAN

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
The Mainichi
(ENGLIŚH LANGUAGE DAILY)
History of 25 Years:
While engaged in its primary mission of the impartial dissemination of
news, the English Mainichi has also since founding in 1922 served as an intel-
lectual bridge between Japan and foreign countries. Toward this end, the
paper has augmented its wide domestic news sources with the services of
the United Press Association of America, Agence France-Presse, and other
foreign agencies.
Largest Paid Circulation in Japan:
Having the largest paid circulation of any English language publication
in Japan, the English Mainichi occupies a unique position in the Japanese
journalistic world.
Highly Popular among Intellectual Circles:
The English Mainichi is highly popular among educated English read-
ing Japanese, students and foreign residents both here and abroad. In recent
days the newspaper has carried exclusively the original text of Winston
Churchill's War Memories and Joseph C. Grew's "Ten Years in Japan."
Send 1st Correspondent to U.S.A. and Britain
The Mainichi Press furthermore was the first Japanese newspaper to
send a staff correspondent to the United States and Britain since the end of
war.
