ACTION
is assigned to
THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
United States Political Adviser
for Japan
Tokyo, February 1l, 1949
No. 88
UNCLASSIF IED (For Department use only)
Subject: Japanese Newspaper Reactions for Week Ending February 10,
4
1949
S
894.9111/
2
C.I
TheHonorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
I have the honor to refer to the Hission's despatch no. 71
of February 4, 1949 and to previous despatches on the above sub-
ject, and to transmit a summary of Japanese newspaper reactions
to current events for the week of February 4 through February 10,
1149
1949.
Summary
Editorial attention of Tokyo newspapers has covered a wide
variey of topics during the past week including the reply of
Secretary of State ACHESON to the STALIN peace offer, the visit
to Japan of Secretary of the Army Kenneth C. ROYALL and his
party, with some emphasis on the statement made here by Assistant
Secretary of Labor GIBSON. Local topics of principal interest
have been the political maneuverings leading to the formation of
the tnird YOHIDA Cabinet, and the conditions and prospects for
food crops.
Acheson Reply to Stalin
Asahi's discussion of the reply made by Secratary of State
Acheson to Stalin's unofficial suggestions for a meeting with
President TRUMAN showed an appreciation of Mr. Acheson's point
that discussions should be through the United Nations, and stated,
".ie consider this a most just and proper attitude."
Mainichi missed the point of Secretary Acheson's statement
that composition of differences must be through the United Nations.
It did
UNCLASSIFTED
DMR
19:3
e

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 88,
February 11, 1949
-2-
It did, however, grasp the idea that if Stalin desires peace, "he
must show it by his conduct." rather than by words.
Visit of Secretary Royall
Additional editorial comment on the Secretary of the Army's tour
of the Far East followed the path already worn smooth by earlier
speculations. Discussion of the position of labor in Japan by Assistant
Secretary of Labor Gibson prompted analytical comment in Mainichi and
Asahi. The former declared: "Although it may appear that government,
capital and labor were respectively severely criticized for their
conduct, what runs through Mr. Gibson's comments is the strong desire
that the role of labor unions in a democratic country be properly
recognized, and that labor unions be democratized and independent...
However, Mr. Gibson warned against the abuse of the right to strike
which had caused labor to 'go too far.' This point should be remembered
by labor unions." Mainichi added, however, that management, likewise,
"must be ready to make sacrifices." Both these newspapers underscored
Mr. Gibson's view that the National Public Service Law would require
revision. Asahi considered that "it is natural for labor unions to
demand that the sacrifices resulting from the enforcement of the nine
economic principles (for economic stabilization) be shared equally by
capit al and labor," and concluded that "to resort to a reckless exercise
of the right to strike, as was the case in the past, in an attempt to
push their demands, is to destroy the foundations of economic stabiliza-
tion." Asahi pleaded for self-restraint by labor unions in the use of
the strike weapon and "struggle tactics," and found that Mr. Gibson's
statement should be understood as "leaving as free a margin as possible
for the development of labor unions, while relying upon the proper
judgment of Japan's workers."
Purge Appeals Committee Appointments
Editorial comment is still lacking on Premier Yoshida's forming
a new Purge Appeals Committee, but noncommittal news stories have
followed its development, including an official statement that "the
government does not intend to alter fundamentally the basic principles
of the purge." Seikei Joho (a newsletter circulated to private sub-
scribers) linked Premier Yoshida's intentions to strengthen the Socialist
Party as a cushion between his conservative Democratic Liberal Party
and the st rong Communist Party opposition in the House of Representatives
and his moves to establish a Purge Appeals Committee. It stated: "It
seems that Premier Yoshida is contemplating the purge clearance of
KAWAKAMI Jotaro and KONO Mitsu as a means of strengthening the Socialist
Party."
Cabinet Composition
Political commentators in many newspapers have covered a wide
range of alternatives facing Premier Yoshida in the choice of his
cabinet
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 88,
February 11, 1949
-3
cabinet ministers. Discussions have dealt with details of interest
principally to Japanese readers, but major newspapers have con-
sistently advised Yoshida to adhere to a one-party composition of
his cabinet and not to attempt any conservative coalition. Mainichi
also emphasized the general proposition that the cabinet should
include "fresh men." It stated: "The DLP (Premier Yoshida's Demo-
cratic Liberal Party) is very unpopular abroad. If the DLP nominates
boss-like, out-of-date statesmen to posts of cabinet ministers, the
new Yoshida cabinet will not be heartily accepted by other nations
or by the people. By 'fresh men' we mean men of fresh opinion and
political sense." Dai Ichi Shimbun said that "not one person suspected
of being involved in any of the recent scandals must join the Cabinet."
Jiji Shimpo felt that Premier Yoshida has occasionally been "off the
track" in personnel matters, and stated that "political history shows
that in Japan all parties which gained an absolute majority in the
Diet split afterwards because of intra-party conflicts which were,
in almost all cases, due to collisions of sentiment rather than dis
parity of opinion concerning policy." Jiji Shimpo therefore advleed
Premier Yoshida to be "cautious" in his "personnel affairs."
Weather and Food Supply
The weather and the farmers' completion of their rice quota were
prominently played. Mainichi pointed out that the mild winter this
year in Japan has been quite abnormal and considered that the winter
wheat crop might be reduced by as much as 50,000,000 bushels, aug-
menting by that amount the usual Japanese annual shortage of 100,000,000
bushels of grain. Stating that nothing is so "unreliable as predictions
regarding the weather," it observed tartly that "this is no time for
the Agriculture Forestry Ministry to rely on what is unreliable."
This newspaper blamed the Ministry for failure to provide insect con-
trols during the winter and advised all its readers to look to their
home vegetable gardens. Nihon Keizai came to the same conclusion
after deploring the "freakish" weather which has so far produced the
warmest winter here since 1932. This newspaper stated that lumbering
in Hokkaido and the Tohoku district has been severely affected by
the lack of snow, lumber being brought out normally on sleds. It
feared a water shortage detrimental to the spring-plarted rice crop
and noted that agar-agar, nori (an edible seaweed), frozen bean curd
and the dried radish industries had already been severely affected.
It stated that the lumber shortage would begin to be felt in April
and thereafter.
Yomiuri played prominently the fulfillment of rice delivery
quotas by Japanese farmers in a detailed tabulation prefecture by
prefecture. It reported that the rice quotas were, as of February 7,
1949, met 100.12% fifty days earlier than last year, and stated:
"The life of austerity and earnest efforts of farmers all over the
nat ion account for this excellent record." Asahi, without troubling
to compliment the farmers, ascribed the results to "thoroughgoing
operation
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 88,
February 11, 1949
-4-
operation of the advance allocations system as well as to the rich
harvest." It stated that Japanese farners would be asked to de
liver another 1,300,000 koka (one koku equals about 5.12 bushels) at
the government-established price of three times that for ordinary
quota delivery. Asahi stated, "At present the black market price
of rice is lower than the three-fold price in most farming villages.
Therefore, farmers want to deliver over-quota rice to the Government
rather than sell it through dangerous illegal channels. Only the
taxation problem often prevents them from doing so." As a way out
of this dilemma, this newspaper considered that since "farmers have a
morbid fear of taxation," it would be preferable "to fix a tax-free
rice price even though it is lower than the three-fold price." Asahi
also noted that the warm winter weather endangers the wheat crop and
that delayed food rationing may greatly affect the Economic Stabiliza
tion Program.
Repeal of Color Bar in United States Laws
All metropolitan newspapers on February 10 carried foreign press
dispatches reporting the unanimous approval given by the House Judi-
ciary Committee to the bill sponsored by Representative Walter H.
JUDD to repeal provi sions in the laws of the United States barring
Asiatics from immigration and naturalization. On February 9, Cabinet
Secretary SATO issued an official statement "rejoicing" at the action
of the Judiciary Committee. After expressing the hope that the bill
would soon be approved by Congress, he stated that this action "is
proof that the Occupation forces have understood the actual situation
in Japan and that all the American people entertain special good will
toward Japan. We hereby express our heartfelt thanks for this American
kindness." In contrast to the unanimous news play on foreign dispatches
on the subject, only Jiji Shimpo carried the Sato statement.
Respectfully yours,

FE
to current events for the week of February 1l through February 17,

INFO
DCR
0
Summary
OLI
Fonnation of the third YOSHIDA cabinet and ancillary political
matters commanded the bulk of news and editorial ati ntion in the
vernacular press during the period under review: On one day, the
SORGE spy case released by the Department of the Army in Washington
captured lead play. News play and editorial attention to speculative
stories quoting a high official American source on the strategic
value of Japan to the United States have been consistently subordinated
to local politics.
Japan's Position in United States Strategy
Clippings and three editorials reflecting the local press treat-
ment accorded the alleged off--the-record statements of a high American
military authority regarding Japan's position in United States strategy
were transmitted to the Department as enclosures to this Mission's
despatch no. 102 of February 17, 1949. In addition to the editorials
under reference, an editorial in Sekai Keizai has come to the attention
of this Mission. This newspaper considered that an important point
brought
UNCLASSIFIED
MAY 4 1945
DMR
ACTION COPY
RETURN TO DC/R FILES WITHIN 14 DAYS, WITH A NOTATION OF ACTION TAKEN

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 108,
February 18, 1949.
-2-
brought out by the discussion of Japan's strategic position is that
Japan's security depends on foreign powers. Saying that Japan's
defeat had "left a blank spot on the political map of the Far East,"
Sekai Keizai continued: "If Japan is to be independent politically
as well as economically and spiritually, she, as a peaceful country
without armament, must achieve strong domestic cooperation, Moreover,
it is desirable that Japan, as a country with a constitution which
renounces war, be courageous enough to head the world's peace move-
ment, and this with sincere determination." It hoped for speedy
stabilization throughout the world and non-involvement in another war,
and added: "We sustain the hope that peace measures realistic enough
to fill the void caused by Japan's defeat in the politico-economic
map of the Far East will be formulated quickly."
Japanese newspapers have in general emphasized the single point
that United States troops would be withdrawn from Japan should stra-
tegic necessities so dictate.
Yoshida Cabinet Formation
The newspapers have followed with close attention the political
maneuvers engaging the energies of leaders in the Democratic Liberal
and in the De ocratic Parties to form a coalition cabinet. Premier
Shigeru Yoshida's past and present attitudes have come in for searching
examination, Efforts of Ken INUKAI, President of the Democratic Party,
to have his party represented in the cabinet despite a party caucus
decision to the contrary have been carefully followed. Announcement
of the cabinet's membership in the morning papers of February 17 prompted
a spate of analytical and admonitory editorials.
Dai Ichi Shimbun (Tokyo daily of small circulation) saw a dark
future for democracy in Japan. "The question is," Dai Ichi stated,
"whether it is permissible in a democracy for a few individuals to
insist on their own personal positions. It is undeniable that up to
the present, Premier Yoshida's whims have been obeyed without resist-
ance. This, however, is a matter of serious reflection, for wherever
men respond to a single powerful person the danger of fascism is al-
ready present." This newspaper continued: "The personal good faith
of Premier Yoshida has nothing to do with the issue. What is to be
questioned is the attitude of Yoshida, who is unwilling to listen to
the opinions of the masses. Let us quote here a statement he made to
United Press Manager HOBERECHT: 'Japan is now under occupation. But
the people naturally dislike to do everything under foreign guidance.
I have done all I could to cooperate with SCAP. But, on the other hand,
in enforoing my policies I have always respected the popular psychology."
Dai Ichi commented that Yoshida had taken advantage of popular psychology
to cat er to the people and that this was one of the reasons for his
popularity.
Hochi Shimbun
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 108,
February 18, 1949.
-3-
Hochi Shimbun (Tokyo daily of small circulation connected
with Yomiuri Shimbun) also found Premier Yoshida highhanded. Re-
viewing a declaration of policy made by Yoshida to foreign cor-
respondents, Hochi Shimbun stated that "in perucing the Premier's
statement we cannot fail to note his usual bold, self-righteous
attitude." This newspaper also criticized the alleged plan of
his for transferring national monopolies of railways and tobacco
to private control, as a means of enticing foreign investment, on
the ground that "this problem is of national interest and the
Premier should respect the will of the Diet in some way."
Mainichi regretted the wasting of a week between Yoshida's
designation as Premier and the formation of his cabinet due to
"clumsy" attempts at coalition with the Democratic Party, and
stated that the Cabinet should "settle down to governing." It
stated that the character of the present cabinet would be reflected
in its execution of the nine-point program (United States interim
directive) for economic stabilization which Mainichi stated is
"an imperative decree." This newspaper considered that economic
stabilization has two phases, economy and labor, and recalled
Assistant Secretary of Labor GIBSON's view that labor is the core
of the matter. "The labor problem is just what the conservative
parties, especially the Democratic Liberal Party, find most difficult
to handle," it stated, and recalled Yoshida's "disastrous phrase
:refractory lackeys'" uttered during his first cabinet.
Yomiuri considered it highly significant that "this is the
first powerful administration since the war's end and the first
based on an absolute majority in the Diet." It believed that it
did not "over-state the case when we say that our future depends
upon the outcome of our national effort in the next yar or two."
In that connection, Yomiuri noted that some opinion eus a difference
between the nine-point economic principles enunciated in General
MACARTHUR's letter to Premier Yoshida and the policies of the Demo-
cratic Liberal Party, but considered that "the nine principles
represent a flexible framework" within which "the order of execution
and relative emphasis are in no sense immutable."
Tokyo Shimbun noted that "the keynote of public pledges (by
the Democratic Liberals) is free economy while various emergency
programs directed by the Occupation authorities presuppose stronger
controls." It found that the success of the Yoshida Government
would be its "ability to compromise the differences between the
two programs."
Asahi not ed the apparent strength of the Democratic Liberal
Party
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 1os,
February 18, 1949.
-4-
Party in the Diet, plus the support of the Democratic repre-
sentatives, but feared that intra-party rivalries threaten and
said; "Unless Premier Yoshida consolidates his party as a first
step, the apparently powerful cabinet may prove to be a weak
one." Asahi stated in conclusion: "The thing that the conserva-
tive parties  charged with the task of settling inflation and
establishing a self-supporting economy -- should do is to effect
these con servative policies which the people really want and
carry them out with courage instead of devoting efforts to con-
servative (political) collaboration (maneuvers) alone."
Respectfully yours,

Ward Price Interview with General MacArthur
Only one editorial had time to appear curing the sericd uncur
review on the sensational report by the Britien journalist G. Ward
Price of his interview with General Douglas Yacirthur, It aooeared
in Mainichi, which is the prineipal client of the Uhited Press, the
agency which distributed the Price story.
Mainichi backed into the topic by first recalling that Japan at
one time tried to imitate Creat Britain und construet an island empi
"unaware that imperialism was already waning to sive place to a new
order". It then recalled that Japan hud tried te imitate Ttaly and
Germey whereupon, according to Mainichi, "the Jaranese peonle learned
the foolishness of aping, for the first time." This newsoaper siied
significantly: "Today no Japanese aspires to imitate the Amerie ns
or the Russians. The defeated Japanese learred to knev thoms-Ives
and their limited capabilities. A part of th- Japanese intelligentsia
gradually began to picture in their minds the small and peaceful
country of Switzerland."
This newspaper quoted General MacArthur's comoarison of Japan
to Switzerland and stated: "This is the first time that a resnonsible
Allied authority as spoken on the future status of Janan in conorete
terms."
Mainichi recognized two trends of thought among the Jananese:
one represented by the opportunists planning to join the stronger in
case of war with a view to regaining lost territories. "Thess people
must have been disappointed by General MacArthur's present tatement,"
it said. "The other thought was re oresented by those whe ned on
leaving everything to the United States in the event of v hey
themselves remaining spectators. These people must be reninded of
the real significance of neutrality and the responsibility it invelves."
Mainichi compared the difficulties encountered by Portuval, Spai,
Sweden, Denmark, Norway, Finland and Switzerland in tryins vo renann
neutral and conduded: "In order that we may maintain neutrality for
a long period to come, it is necessary that each member of the nation
sincerely resolve to defond peace and refrain from all seltish ambi-
tions. We must remember that only our own firm determination will
entitle us to the rivileges of neutrality."

Nichi Nichi (connected with Mainichi) in its comment on t
Ward Price reportage made the two points that Japan must reconsurugt

destiny of Japan. This is a very important matter. If they
hate to he communist-controlled, the taak of checking communim

LSB White Paper
The white paper on Japanese economy published by the Economic

in the Big Three, in Nihon Keizai, and in Tokyo Shimbun.

I have the honor to refer to the Mission's despatch
no. 176 of March 25, 1949 and to previous despatches on the
above subject and to transmit herewith a summary of Japanese
newspaper reactions to current events for the week of March 25
through March 31, 1949.
49
Summary.
C
The bulk of editorial coment was devoted--as it had been
for the previous fortnight--to the Japanese budget and attendant
problems such as taxation and the majority party's abandonment
of its campaign pledges. The only other topic attracting a
significant volume of editorial discussion was peace negotiations
in China.
China Peace.
Extreme pessimism marked all editorial discussions of
prospects of peace between the Nationalist Government and the
communists in China.
Yomiuri considered that "peace on an equal footing is gen-
erally believed to be exceedingly difficult of achievement" and
"since the
UNCLASSIFIED
MAY 201010
AOTION COPY
TO.DC/R FILES WITHIN 14 DAYS, WITH A NOTATION OF ACTION TAKEN.

=== Page 226 === (confidence 1.00)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 195,
April 1, 1949.
-2-
"since the communists are said to stick to their announced
terms, there is little hope for peace." This newspaper con-
sidered that "reorganization of the national army and the
punishment of war criminals are the biggest issues."
Asahi and Jiji Shimpo concurred that the principles of
the Chinese communists have changed from advocacy of "new
democracy" to the orthodox communist line. Asahi said: "The
communists' decision is featured as a shift in party principle
from a new democracy to socialisn. These changes in basic
policies have tended to darken the prospects for peace talks.
The new democracy which was proposed by MAo Tse-tung in Janu-
ary 1940 is said to be one of the most important revolutionary
ideas published out side Moscow since the Russian Revolution.
The principle of new democracy was formed during World War II
when the communists felt the need to compromise with the demo-
cratic powers and to encourage the development of popular fronts
in Europe...That the Chinese communists have relinquished the
principle of new democracy shows they have developed to the point
where they monopolize political power and that the principle of
new democracy has already completed its role, even in China."
Jiji Shimpo also noted this shift and commented that "To date
they (the communists) have advocated a new democracy and given
the impression of having shelved their communist principles.
Now, however, they have brought their true character to the
fore. In addition to being a decisive blow to. those who held
to the illusion that the Chinese communists would become similar
to Tito's communists, this fact gives us the feeling that the
peace negotiations--in the face of the determination of the
Chinese communists--are practically hopeless."
Nihon Keizai believed that since the communists insist on
their eight-point program for peace, "this means virtual sub-
mission and is therefore not acceptable to the Nanking Government
which is ready to end the civil war with a 'glorious peace."
This newspaper considered that "the success of the peace confer-
ence depends on the balance between the Nationalists and the
communists."
Budget.
The necessity of subcrdinating Democratic Liberal Party
canpaign pledges to the necessities of economic stabilization
as required
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 195,
April 1, 1949.
-3-
as required by this Headquarters was the theme of several
editorials, mainly repetitive of arguments already covered
in previous despatches. Yomiuri, however, found some comfort
in pointing out that some of those pledges can be fulfilled,
notably the reopening of eating and drinking establishments,
abolition of control over daily necessities and the curtailing
of government personnel.
In one of the most trenchant editorials noted to date,
Tokyo Times said that "the importance of the nine-point economic
stabilization progran was not sufficiently recognized" at the
time of its enunciation. It continued by making two points.
The first was that increasing taxes are to "plug inflationary
loopholes by restricting or banning construction bonds, Recon-
struction Finance Bank loans and local government bonds; and
simultaneously make the people of Japan recognize the actual
condition of Japan's economy by charging import subsidies to
tax revenue instead of to foreign aid." The second point was
that by creating a counterpart fund in yen for foreign aid, the
way is opened for industrial and other long-term loans. Tokyo
Times concluded that some enterprises might collapse and unem-
ployment increase but that "the national economy cannot be
sound before it goes through this stage" of deflation.
Yomiuri and Jiji Shimpo both doubted that the anticipated
revenues from taxes could be collected. Jiji Shimpo considered
that "in so far as the earned income tax is concerned, little
difficulty is seen in its collection" since it is deducted from
payrolls by the employer. However, this newspaper felt that in
the case of self-assessed taxes "which represent two thirds of
the entire income tax, especially in view of the business income
tax, it is quite doubtful whether the revenues from these taxes
will reach the anticipated sum." Yomiuri, without going into so
much detail, stated: "Whether this large revenue will be secured
under the existing conditions of tax collection is questionable."
Respectfully yours,
For the Acting Political Adviser:
Cloyce K. Huston
Counselor of Mission

Subject: Japanese Newspaper Reactions for the Week Ending

Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Yission's despatch no. 21l of
pril 3, 1949 and to previcus despatches on the ahove subject and to
transmit herewith a surmary of newspaper reactions to current events for
the week of April 8 through April 14, 1949.
Summary
Primary emphasis was given to domestic affairs during the period
under review. The fourth annual convention of the Socialist Party.
opening in Tokyo on April 14 rated the best play, followed by attention
to the status of women in Japan. Sporadic editorials discussed peace
efforts in China.
Premier Yoshida's remarks on neutrality (reported in the Mission's
despatch no. 213 of April 11, 1949) were censured by Asahi but no other
editorial comment on this subject has been noted.
Socialist Party Convention
The Social Democratic Party's defeat in the January 1949 elections
in which it dropped - in terms of representatives elected - from first
to third place among Japanese parties, was a dramatic event stimulating
much discussion and analysis (see the Mission's despatches nos. 58 of
January 28, 1949 and 71 of February 4, 1949). Effort of the Socialist
Party to pull itself into a cohesive force at its fourth annual conven-
tion opening in Tokyo on April 14, 1949 has prompted Toko editorialists
o diuss again the party's plight.
The problem facing the socialists was expressed sharply by Nihon
"It is feared that the fourth national convention will intensify
UNCLASSIFIED
FILOD
ACTION COPY

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch no. 230,
April 15, 1949.
-2-
Socialist intra-party differences rather than reconstruct the party.
This is premised on the fact that the basic policies to be decided at.
this convention are the product of the ideologies of both the factions,
left and right, incompatible since the formation of the Socialist
Party, and of the differences in their characters. The feud is an
outgrowth of history and basic in type. It stems from more serious
differences in ideology and character than the feud among the conserva-
tives who have common interests.
"The first problem concerns the policies for party structure and
activities. The Socialist leftist faction!insists upon original
socialism or scientific socialism, in opposition to the present progres-
sive social democracy, and upon the party's assumption of leadership
over the working populace. Cn t e other hand, the rightist faction
contends that the socialism upon which the Socialist Party was formed
stands on the same principles as the British Labor Party which advocates
non-Marxism or the second international."
Most editorialists make the point that an important problem to be
solved is the cuestion of who will assume the party's leadership. But
several admonish the party for emphasizing too much the ideological,
the theoretical and the abstract. Asahi stated in this respect:
"If responsibility (for defeat at the election of January 23,
1949) is to be argued for the sake of party reconstruction, the probe
should go deep; otherwise the intended reccnstruction would end in
one of expediency with the next general election as the target. It
must be added, however, that fresh leadership equal to the task of
reconstruction, coupled with the elimination of boss-dominated factions,
is the first and foremost requirement.
"Further, the campaign policies reportedly involve such problems
as a revolutionary formula, scientific socialism, theories of social
democracy, a class political party devoted to action, and a class-
conscious general mass party. It is admitted that this is a list of
major theoretical problems. In the eyes of outsiders, however, they
are nothing but topics of factional rivalry disguised as theoretical
conflicts."
Nichi Nichi took a similar position: "We expect the Socialist Party,
above all, to become a party based on reformists' idealism. Socialists
should be administrators of social justice, facing squarely the actual
situation in Japan. The question of ideology is secondary. They should
be consolidated on a moral basis on the one hand and on the other they
should effect a united front. If they fail to fulfil these two conditions,
their good ideas and lofty ideals will prove meaningless. There is only
one way to end the useless strife now going on. They should stop
quarreling over imported ideology and stand on the solid ground of
reality." Mainichi called for unity and definite programming: "Of
primary importance are a full understanding of the principle on which
the party was founded, the maintenance of a spint of reform and the
formation of definite programs." Tokyo Shimbun considered that it is
"of first importance to the Socialist Party, in rebuilding its
organization, to show a unified plan of practical action in its policies,
personnel
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch no. 230,
April 15, 1949.
-3-
persornel (i.e. leadership), organization and party program. As long
as the party presents its ideological conflicts as pertaining to the
foundations of its existence and to the struggle between right and
left-wing factions for party hegemony, the Socialist Party will never
succeed in getting back on its feet."
Yoshida Doubts on Neutrality
Asahi was moved on April 12 to censure Premier Yoshida for his
reply on April 8 to a Parliamentary question, in which he expressed
doubt as to whether it was "proper" for Japan to maintain neutrality
permanently (Mission's despatch no. 213 of April 11, 1949). Asahi
stated: "This answer is regrettable. Needless to say the problem
of neutrality is a very serious one. The reply was not made too
seriously, nor to our surprise vas there strong resentment against
Lis response." This newspaper considered that the maintenance of
neutrality depended on geographical position, unity of the people,
elements of time, neighboring states, the world situation and the
historical moment. It considered that Premier Yoshida's example of
Pelgiun whose neutrality was twice violated was inept: "What about
Switzerland?", it asked, and warned that missing this opportunity to
establish Japanese neutrality would be an iirep rable mistake. "This
neutrality cannot be maintained merely on the strength of a declaration.
It can only succeed if we put our trust in the justice and faith of
the other nations of the world cnd be legally recognized by them. Have
we no right to claim their recognition?"
Status of Women
Celebration of Women's Week, from April 10 to April 17, 1949,
marking the third anniversary of women's suffrage in Japan, has prompted
a  editorials on the subject. "Be ambitious," Asahi advised, but
felt that women would not be "emancipated by the mere observation of
women's week once a year, or by employing what we may call abstract
slogans." Kainichi asked for the improvement of the "intellectual
level" in women rather than "an offensive against men." Yomiuri
regretted that prostitution was still permitted "almost openly" in
Japan and that the stipulations of the Labor Standards Law of equal
Wages for equal work were being "ignored" in most occupations. "In
the present campaign" Yomiuri considered, "we are led to feel that
women's week is merely a fete of intellectual women. It is hoped that
steady efforts will be made to undertake more practical problems
instead of idealistic ones as is the case at present."
Respectfully yours,
For the Acting Political Adviser:
Cloyce K. Huston
Counselor of Mission

China War Renewal
Tokyo newspapers now seem convinced that the Communists will
control all of China. Jini Shimpe however considered thet the Com-
mun cts blundered when "they betrayed the people's confidence by

Newspaper treatment of news showed no definite pattern during
the week under review but covered a wide variety of topics. Best
single news play was gven to the United States presentation of
Its views to the Far Eastern Commission on the quest ion of Japanese
participation in int ernational conferences, thus demonstrating
widespread int erest, though prompting only two editorials in small
cir:ulation dailies. Prospects of lifting the Berlin blcckade
ranked second among foreign stories. The coal strike and the
arrival of Dr. Carl W. SHOUP in Teky to review the Japanese taxa-
tior syst em were the domestic stories played most prominently.
Japanoce Participation in International Conferences
Yukan Chugai applauded the presentation of the United States
views to the Far Easten Cemmission on the relaxation of rules
governing Japanese part!cipation in international conferences.
This daily

This daily found that "in order to lay the foundation for an un-
shakable world peace based cn the readjusted postwar relations
between the nations of the world, we judge it to be a democratic
move and a wise pcliey to have Japan's ideas and wishes reflected
in international affairs." It recognized that "some member nations
of the Far Eastern Commission are not pleased" but considered that
the day would come when this "fair and sound move of the United
States" would be understood. Nihon Keizai made the telling point
that having been out of touch with the world for so long the Jap-
anese viewpoint had become "narrow and distorted" and that "if we
understand how each natien in the werld is struggling against
economic difficulties we shall realize how important our responsi-
bility is." Admitting that the opposition of certain member nations
within the Far Eastern Commission "is not necessarily incomprehensible
to us", this newspaper considered that the United States proposal
was made in reccgnition of Japan s efforts to become a "peaceful.
cultural and demccratic nation as set forth in the new Constitution."
Ycmiuri placed a broader interpretation on the presentation cf
the United States views to the Far Eastern Ccmmission than did its
contemporaries, since it linked them with the Iifting cf the Berlin
blockade, fer the sake of restoring the world's trade. "Hew agnificant
this new international atmosphere is to Japan, nich is laboring to
attain a self sufficient econcmy as its major goal, nee not be re-
iterated" it said. and pointed out that self sufficieney for Japan
means obtaining through exports sufficient funds to pay for imports
of food and raw materials. It then said boldly:
"The difficulty of this task under existing conditiens is not
fully realized by the public. In addition to focd imports,
nearly half c: the presently impcrted industrial materials
is paid for by the United States taxpayer. If a major part
of this aid is diverted to the production of consumer goods .
through lack of planning -- and economic revival in the proper
sense of the word is not promoted, the United States taxpayer
will naturally become doubtful as to the effests of his burden.
We, too, should not expect that such aid will be given for long.
"The report that the United States Gevernment, which attaches the
greatest importance to the attainment of Japanese self-sufficiency,
is preparing a new phase of Occupation policy affords an important
hint on this peint, regardless of whether it is related to the
new intemational atmosphere or not. Free participation in
international economic egreements is not an immediate possibility.
However, joining the Intemational Monetary Fund will be realized
bafore long. If this is realized, credits from the World Bank will
come up for discussion."

Shoup Tax Mission
Reorganization of the taxation syst em in Japan was recognized
by most editorialists as the preferalle way of eliminating inequalities
in laxation and possibly li htening the burden. Yukan Chugai put the
problem succ inetly: "If tax dodging can be prevented, it will be
possible to lighten tie lax burden os the general piblic by raising
the minimum taxable. d th de ndut exe t ions in the income
tex schedules, and b lovering e es. In short, in the proposed
rovlsion of the tax s'ste, it is i"ertant to qualize the burden."
Nihon Keiza1 adopted a philosophizing approach. "Opinion varies
on whather the existin: taxation system is capitalistic or anti-
cupitalistic. Wh: p nion Doctor Shoup will express in this regard
demand: our greelest ! ntion." Its analysis of the present taxation
system llamed the curr -over into the postwar of prewar methods. "The
old progressive inaome tax rates winich were adopted in rewer days when
income was net eveni li stributed, still apply today vin income is
almost equal ized. C. sequently, the incentive to earn has been de..
creased and the eccmuliat ion of capilal hindered, since work perforaed
above : certain

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 311,
May 14, 1949.
-5-
above a certain level results in only a negligible inerement of
income. It is cnly natural to give socialistic consideration to
the capitalistic tax system. The fundamental cause of the con-
fusion in the present taxation system is that it is net based
on clear-cut ideas."
Jiji Shimpo compared income taxes before and after the war.
"In 1930 a man with an annual income of 1,500 yen (about 500.00)
per year paid an income tax of only nine yen, or 0.6 per cent of
his income. The sum of 1, 500 yen in 1930 may be compared with
372,030 yen in 1949, on the basis of price indices, and the income
tax on this sum, under the present system, would be a little more
than 135, 200 yen, or 36.3 per cent of the total income." In partial
explenation of this phenomenon, Jiji Shimpo stated that property
taxes which formerly supplied 24 per cent of the total income had
declined to 2.3 per cent of the total.
Mainichi listed several points which it considered needed the
attention of the group of tax experts led by Doctor Shoup. It
listed as first consideration the charge that "the current tax
obligations are too heavy", second, that "while the tax rates in
force are generally exorbitant" a more important element is the
smoothing out of the ineguities of assessment whereby salaried
persons pay their taxes through deductions at the source while
"scoundrels" evade them, third, a clean-up among tax offices and
the elimination of corrupt tax collectors, a situation which it
called "correctable". Tokyo Shimbun emphasized the argument that
present graduated tax rates discourage initiative. "The imposition
of the graduated income tax system under which only a slight profit
can be made beyond a certain income level will discourage an in-
dividual:s will to work." Most editorialists were careful to point
out that any revision of the tax rates must take into account the
commitments of the Government to its halanced budget and its ob-
ligations under the Economic Stabilization Program.

than susiension of the removel of factories on the
refarations list. The rosd to full-scale operation
of designated factories will be opened only when it
is linked with the unrestricted developmentof peace-
ful industries Cur next problem will be how to es-
tablish the new industrial structure without con-
cerning ourselves exclusively with the relief of
bankrupt enterprises."
Nihon Keizai considered that the suspension of repara-
tions will affect each enteririse in e different manner,
some, for instence increesing prcduction immediately while
others will need to make preliminary repairs. It said:
"Because of the prevailing tight money situation, various
difficulties are anticipated in raising funds for repairs
and operation. However, enterprises with designated facto-
ries can now frame reconstruction and adjustment programs"
though it considered that the lack of ready quantities of
raw materials would still militate against rapid increases
in industrial output. Kogyo Shimbun ("Industrial Newspaper")
noted and deprecated the immediate hullishness in machinery
and heavy industry stocks folloviny the announcement, since
it considered that the supply of mechinery now available from
war industries will depress the market for new machinery.
Tax Reform
Three points were made releatedly in editorial discus-
sions of taxes and the reforms in the Japanese taxation
system expected of the Shoup mission now here: first, that
inequities of tax levies be smoothed out, as a result of
which it was anticipated that taxes will be lightened; sec-
ond, in addition to personal income taxes, corporate taxes
need attention; third, corruption - whether by tax dodgers
or tax collectors -- must be eliminated.
A number of subordinate considerations were advanced,
however, together with these more important factors which
have already

have already been noted in the despatch under reference.
Yomiuri stated rather sadly that "mitigation of the gen-
eral tex burden depends upon the government's efforts to
minimize exrenditure". It also delivered a blistering
attack on taxes levied on admissions to museums, zoos,
plays, moving pictures: and on books. It stated: "Under
the circumstances, the 'cultural state' may cynically be
referred to as a miserable state. The creation of high
admission taxes to state and science museums is unreason-
able conduct unparelleled in world history. At present,
the United Nations Economic, Social and Cultural Organiza-
tion is trying to ease international postal rates on books
and publications. However, overnment officials in Japan
do not appreciate the value of books and publications. As
much as baseball and other sports, publications, plays,
cinema. arts, and music should be encouraged by the govern-
ment. But the Japanese Government is hampering their de-
velopment by levying ad taxes on them. Under the circum-
stances, this nation can be regarded as nothing more than
a poor and barbarous state completely devoid of noble
sentiments."
Asahi brought up the question of adequate enforcement
of taxation laws and their reform and enactment, while Jiji
Shimpo hoped that Dr. Shoup would study the "peculiarities
of the Japanse economy". Mainichi recommended that local
finances and the duplication of taxation for national and
local purposes be thorougnly examined, while Asahi pointed
out that community property taxation schedules should take
into account that in Japan the parents of married couples
often live with their children; it added: "In revising
our taxation system, our family system may become involved
to such an extent as to complicate the issue."
Stock Exchange Reopening
Nihon Keizai considered the reopening of the stock
exchange, closed since August 1945, as a "step forward on
the path to economic normalcy" and defined the exchange's
function as the protection of the investing public. Asahi
concurred that the exchange was designed to protect the
"conservative investor" and emphasized that speculative
transactions are discouraged by the present Securities
Transactions Law. Jiji Shimpo felt that the stock exchange
exercises constant supervision over the operation of pri-
vate business, a function which it believed could not be
exercised by government.
